GRE Master Vocabulary Practice Test

Group 1 Practice Test (Words 1–30)

1. In toxic corporate startups, performative buzzwords like "synergy" and "hustle" ________ in every slide deck, masking the absence of any real product.
  • A) belie
  • B) abound
  • C) deify
  • D) flout
  • E) placate
2. Following the sudden corporate restructuring, the marketing division's timeline remained completely ________, lacking defined deliverables or clear leadership roles.
  • A) cerebral
  • B) didactic
  • C) amorphous
  • D) loquacious
  • E) conspicuous
3. Walter White’s ________, unsmiling demeanor as Heisenberg terrified local street dealers accustomed to impulsive chaos.
  • A) austere
  • B) congenial
  • C) insipid
  • D) quixotic
  • E) puerile
4. In romantic partnerships, an overly cheerful demeanor often serves to ________ deep-seated resentment brewing beneath daily interactions.
  • A) deify
  • B) belie
  • C) flout
  • D) placate
  • E) abound
5. In classic Bollywood melodramas, the patriarch’s ________ temperament means one minor misunderstanding can lead to a son being disowned instantly.
  • A) taciturn
  • B) capricious
  • C) didactic
  • D) misanthropic
  • E) homogeneous
6. Rather than relying on gut instinct, the behavioral psychologist took a strictly ________ approach to analyzing cognitive bias and emotional triggers.
  • A) puerile
  • B) humdrum
  • C) cerebral
  • D) quixotic
  • E) spendthrift
7. Kabir was known across the firm as a ________ manager whose warm charisma and inclusive leadership naturally put anxious new hires at ease.
  • A) misanthropic
  • B) congenial
  • C) capricious
  • D) negligent
  • E) taciturn
8. The flashy sports car parked outside the rundown motel was far too ________ for a drug dealer attempting to hide from the DEA.
  • A) insipid
  • B) conspicuous
  • C) amorphous
  • D) cursory
  • E) wary
9. A ________ review of the supplier contract failed to catch the hidden licensing penalties buried on the last page.
  • A) cursory
  • B) didactic
  • C) cerebral
  • D) quixotic
  • E) congenial
10. For a junior analyst, presenting revenue forecasts before the ruthless venture capital committee felt like a ________ ordeal.
  • A) humdrum
  • B) didactic
  • C) daunting
  • D) homogeneous
  • E) loquacious
11. Bollywood fans frequently ________ their favorite superstars, building literal shrines and offering milk libations before new film releases.
  • A) flout
  • B) placate
  • C) belie
  • D) deify
  • E) abjure
12. Viewers found the political satire far too heavy-handed and ________, as it interrupted engaging storylines to deliver moralizing lectures.
  • A) insipid
  • B) didactic
  • C) amorphous
  • D) congenial
  • E) capricious
13. To build grassroots momentum, party organizers used encrypted channels to ________ election rallies directly to undecided swing voters.
  • A) placate
  • B) flout
  • C) disseminate
  • D) deify
  • E) belie
14. Scaling the high-yield chemical synthesis in an underground bunker was barely ________ without industrial-grade ventilation.
  • A) feasible
  • B) didactic
  • C) quixotic
  • D) puerile
  • E) insipid
15. The corrupt minister brazenly ________ safety codes by ordering the immediate construction of the resort over protected wetlands.
  • A) placated
  • B) deified
  • C) flouted
  • D) abounded
  • E) belied
16. The boardroom suffered from severe groupthink because the leadership team was entirely ________, composed only of executives from the same background.
  • A) misanthropic
  • B) homogeneous
  • C) loquacious
  • D) cerebral
  • E) conspicuous
17. After the exhilarating adrenaline of his undercover cartel operation, Hank found the ________ desk job of filing daily DEA paperwork unbearable.
  • A) humdrum
  • B) quixotic
  • C) conspicuous
  • D) didactic
  • E) congenial
18. The focus group rejected the newly formulated soda, calling its synthetic sweetness flat, unmemorable, and utterly ________.
  • A) austere
  • B) loquacious
  • C) insipid
  • D) daunting
  • E) cerebral
19. Jesse's ________ friend Skinny Pete could turn a simple corner-store run into a thirty-minute philosophical monologue.
  • A) taciturn
  • B) loquacious
  • C) austere
  • D) wary
  • E) negligent
20. Disillusioned by corporate greed and ruthless backstabbing, the former CEO retreated into a ________ lifestyle, refusing all human contact.
  • A) misanthropic
  • B) congenial
  • C) puerile
  • D) quixotic
  • E) spendthrift
21. Calling the company's aggressive, 80-hour-work-week policy "Workplace Wellness" was an offensive ________ that angered overworked staff.
  • A) proclivity
  • B) misnomer
  • C) spendthrift
  • D) affectation
  • E) platitude
22. Leaving confidential user passwords unencrypted in plain text was an inexcusably ________ decision that exposed millions of accounts.
  • A) austere
  • B) negligent
  • C) didactic
  • D) cerebral
  • E) wary
23. The authoritarian leader preferred ________ sycophants who praised his every decree over competent advisors who told him hard truths.
  • A) misanthropic
  • B) obsequious
  • C) taciturn
  • D) austere
  • E) wary
24. In an attempt to ________ striking gig workers before the quarterly shareholder meeting, the app rolled out minor fuel reimbursements.
  • A) placate
  • B) flout
  • C) deify
  • D) belie
  • E) disseminate
25. High-stakes traders often exhibit a natural ________ for risk-taking, finding comfort in volatile swings that would terrify average investors.
  • A) misnomer
  • B) proclivity
  • C) decorum
  • D) spendthrift
  • E) affectation
26. When asked about dropping stock valuations, the executive lost his temper and resorted to ________ tantrums and childish name-calling.
  • A) cerebral
  • B) puerile
  • C) austere
  • D) didactic
  • E) congenial
27. Building a clean, ethical empire out of illegal narcotics distribution was Walter White's most tragic, ________ delusion.
  • A) humdrum
  • B) insipid
  • C) quixotic
  • D) negligent
  • E) homogeneous
28. Having won a massive lottery jackpot, the young ________ blew through his fortune in eighteen months by hosting lavish celebrity parties.
  • A) miscreant
  • B) spendthrift
  • C) curmudgeon
  • D) neophyte
  • E) polymath
29. Unlike his flamboyant, boastful partners, Mike Ehrmantraut was famously ________, speaking only when a tactical warning was absolutely necessary.
  • A) loquacious
  • B) taciturn
  • C) puerile
  • D) obsequious
  • E) capricious
30. Having been burnt by fraudulent crypto exchanges before, institutional investors grew exceptionally ________ of unregulated offshore platforms.
  • A) wary
  • B) spendthrift
  • C) insipid
  • D) didactic
  • E) humdrum

Group 1 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. B) abound — To exist in large quantities[span_0](start_span)[span_0](end_span).
  2. C) amorphous — Lacking structure, shape, or clear form[span_1](start_span)[span_1](end_span).
  3. A) austere — Strict, dour, stern, or unadorned in character[span_2](start_span)[span_2](end_span).
  4. B) belie — To give a false impression of; conceal or contradict[span_3](start_span)[span_3](end_span).
  5. B) capricious — Fickle, unpredictable, or having volatile moods[span_4](start_span)[span_4](end_span).
  6. C) cerebral — Analytical, rational, and focused on intellect rather than emotion[span_5](start_span)[span_5](end_span).
  7. B) congenial — Likable, friendly, and pleasant[span_6](start_span)[span_6](end_span).
  8. B) conspicuous — Obvious, patent, and easily noticed[span_7](start_span)[span_7](end_span).
  9. A) cursory — Hasty, lacking effort, or perfunctory[span_8](start_span)[span_8](end_span).
  10. C) daunting — Intimidating, formidable, or seemingly impossible[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  11. D) deify — Look up to someone as a god; venerate or exalt[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  12. B) didactic — Educational or meant for teaching, sometimes condescendingly[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  13. C) disseminate — Disperse, distribute, or circulate broadly[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  14. A) feasible — Practical, attainable, or likely to be achieved[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  15. C) flout — Scorn, defy, or break a rule without guilt[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  16. B) homogeneous — Composed of similar parts; uniform and alike[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  17. A) humdrum — Dull, mundane, and boring[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  18. C) insipid — Lacking flavor, bland, or lifeless[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).
  19. B) loquacious — Extremely talkative or wordy[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  20. A) misanthropic — Having a deep distaste for humans and society[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  21. B) misnomer — An inaccurate or misleading name[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).
  22. B) negligent — Lacking care, attention, or diligence; remiss[span_21](start_span)[span_21](end_span).
  23. B) obsequious — Excessively submissive, servile, or sycophantic[span_22](start_span)[span_22](end_span).
  24. A) placate — Decrease anger or negative emotions; mollify or pacify[span_23](start_span)[span_23](end_span).
  25. B) proclivity — A fondness, leaning, or natural propensity[span_24](start_span)[span_24](end_span).
  26. B) puerile — Immature, juvenile, and childish[span_25](start_span)[span_25](end_span).
  27. C) quixotic — Excessively visionary, impractical, or utopian[span_26](start_span)[span_26](end_span).
  28. B) spendthrift — A profligate squanderer who spends recklessly[span_27](start_span)[span_27](end_span).
  29. B) taciturn — Reserved, unforthcoming, or speaking very little[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span).
  30. A) wary — Vigilant, careful, and exercising caution[span_29](start_span)[span_29](end_span).

Group 2 Practice Test (Words 31–60)

31. To maximize illicit profit margins, the crooked chemist decided to ________ the pure formula with cheap industrial thinning agents.
  • A) advocate
  • B) adulterate
  • C) aggrandize
  • D) castigate
  • E) covet
32. Despite intense pushback from the product team, the engineering director continued to ________ for completely refactoring the legacy codebase.
  • A) advocate
  • B) castigate
  • C) demur
  • D) desiccate
  • E) covet
33. Walter White’s insatiable desire to ________ his criminal reputation led him to reject buyout offers that would have kept his family secure.
  • A) ameliorate
  • B) desiccate
  • C) aggrandize
  • D) demur
  • E) construe
34. When the emergency production alert fired on Slack, the on-call DevOps engineer tackled the server outage with surprising ________.
  • A) diatribe
  • B) decorum
  • C) alacrity
  • D) calumny
  • E) deftness
35. After years in high-stakes finance, Aryan felt ________ about quitting his lucrative partnership to launch a risky startup.
  • A) ambivalent
  • B) avaricious
  • C) brazen
  • D) contrite
  • E) craven
36. The HR director hoped that introducing flexible hybrid hours would ________ employee dissatisfaction regarding return-to-office mandates.
  • A) desiccate
  • B) ameliorate
  • C) castigate
  • D) aggrandize
  • E) covet
37. The junior developer was remarkably ________ to feedback, revising his entire pull request without becoming defensive.
  • A) amenable
  • B) caustic
  • C) anachronistic
  • D) avaricious
  • E) craven
38. Carrying a heavy physical ledger into a modern cloud architecture sprint planning meeting seemed delightfully ________.
  • A) brazen
  • B) anachronistic
  • C) contrite
  • D) deft
  • E) ingenuous
39. Leaving a high-paying corporate law firm to join an unproven political campaign was an ________ leap that stunned his family.
  • A) audacious
  • B) banal
  • C) craven
  • D) ingenuous
  • E) anachronistic
40. The cartel boss was notorious for his ________ appetite, executing loyal lieutenants simply to hoard a larger share of the territory.
  • A) contrite
  • B) avaricious
  • C) ingenuous
  • D) banal
  • E) amenable
41. Critics panned the big-budget Bollywood action film for its ________ dialogue and formulaic revenge tropes.
  • A) deft
  • B) banal
  • C) audacious
  • D) caustic
  • E) incredulous

  • A) brazen
  • B) benign
  • C) caustic
  • D) avaricious
  • E) craven
43. The corrupt minister made the ________ move of attending the anti-graft press conference wearing luxury watches purchased with diverted state funds.
  • A) benign
  • B) contrite
  • C) brazen
  • D) craven
  • E) deft
44. During the heated municipal election, rival campaign aides resorted to vile ________, fabricating affairs to tarnish the mayor’s reputation.
  • A) decorum
  • B) calumny
  • C) alacrity
  • D) diatribe
  • E) demur
45. An effective therapist creates a non-judgmental space where patients feel comfortable offering ________ accounts of their domestic struggles.
  • A) candid
  • B) craven
  • C) banal
  • D) caustic
  • E) anachronistic
46. The CEO took to the podium to ________ the rogue sales division for falsifying quarterly revenue numbers.
  • A) covet
  • B) ameliorate
  • C) castigate
  • D) aggrandize
  • E) construe
47. Jesse wilted under Walter's ________ insults, unable to withstand the constant verbal abuse mocking his intelligence.
  • A) candid
  • B) caustic
  • C) benign
  • D) deft
  • E) contrite
48. In toxic romantic relationships, an insecure partner is apt to ________ even innocent delays in text replies as deliberate betrayals.
  • A) covet
  • B) castigate
  • C) demur
  • D) construe
  • E) desiccate
49. Overcome by genuine remorse after the heated domestic dispute, the husband appeared thoroughly ________ and willing to seek couples counseling.
  • A) contrite
  • B) craven
  • C) brazen
  • D) audacious
  • E) avaricious
50. Navigating the startup's ________ equity distribution agreement required a seasoned venture attorney to decipher the tax loopholes.
  • A) deft
  • B) convoluted
  • C) banal
  • D) candid
  • E) benign

  • A) castigate
  • B) covet
  • C) demur
  • D) desiccate
  • E) ameliorate

  • A) craven
  • B) deft
  • C) candid
  • D) brazen
  • E) benign

  • A) diatribe

  • D) alacrity
  • E) demur

  • A) craven

  • A) aggrandize
  • B) adulterate

  • A) derivative

  • A) ameliorate
  • B) desiccate

  • E) aggrandize

  • A) decorum

  • E) demur

  • A) incredulous
  • B) ingenuous

  • E) benign

  • A) caustic
  • B) ingenuous
  • C) audacious
  • D) brazen
  • E) convoluted

Group 2 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. B) adulterate — Damage the quality of; corrupt or debase with impure additives[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span).
  2. A) advocate — Support, champion, or recommend[span_31](start_span)[span_31](end_span).
  3. C) aggrandize — Enhance one's own power, standing, or reputation[span_32](start_span)[span_32](end_span).
  4. C) alacrity — Eagerness, zeal, or brisk speed[span_33](start_span)[span_33](end_span).
  5. A) ambivalent — Having conflicting emotions or unsure feelings[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span).
  6. B) ameliorate — Improve a bad feeling, condition, or situation[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).
  7. A) amenable — Receptive to change, compliant, or open[span_36](start_span)[span_36](end_span).
  8. B) anachronistic — Belonging to the wrong era or antiquated time[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  9. A) audacious — Bold, intrepid, or unafraid of risks[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  10. B) avaricious — Excessively greedy; rapacious[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  11. B) banal — Unoriginal, trite, and boring[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span).
  12. B) benign — Harmless, gentle, or having little adverse effect[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  13. C) brazen — Shameless, brash, and excessively forward[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  14. B) calumny — Slander, libel, or defamation[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span).
  15. A) candid — Honest, forthright, and blunt[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  16. C) castigate — Scold, rebuke, or berate someone harshly[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  17. B) caustic — Hostile, scathing, and bitter in speech[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  18. D) construe — Understand or parse subjectively[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  19. A) contrite — Remorseful, repentant, or rueful[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  20. B) convoluted — Tortuous, complex, and difficult to follow[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span).
  21. B) covet — Strongly desire, long for, or yearn for[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  22. A) craven — Cowardly or lacking bravery[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  23. C) decorum — Proper behavior, propriety, and politeness[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  24. B) deft — Dexterous, highly proficient, and skillful[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  25. D) demur — Hesitate, object, or express doubt[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  26. A) derivative — Imitative and unoriginal[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  27. B) desiccate — Dry out completely; remove all moisture[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span).
  28. B) diatribe — A forceful, bitter verbal attack or harangue[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span).
  29. A) incredulous — Doubtful, skeptical, and disbelieving[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span).
  30. B) ingenuous — Naive, innocent, and unworldly[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).

Group 3 Practice Test (Words 61–90)

61. Once the public outrage over the server migration began to ________, product managers finally resumed feature rollouts.
  • A) coalesce
  • B) abate
  • C) canonize
  • D) burgeon
  • E) contend
62. Under threat of subpoena, the rogue whistleblower refused to ________ his moral convictions and testify against his team.
  • A) assuage
  • B) abjure
  • C) coalesce
  • D) burgeon
  • E) contend
63. In modern behavioral therapy, sudden and violent mood swings are treated as ________ behaviors requiring diagnostic screening.
  • A) anomalous
  • B) bucolic
  • C) cogent
  • D) copious
  • E) diffident
64. The deep-seated ________ between the rival political dynasts made any cross-party legislative compromise impossible.
  • A) deference
  • B) chicanery
  • C) antipathy
  • D) deference
  • E) canonization
65. High-frequency proprietary trading firms protect their ________ algorithmic codebases behind ironclad non-disclosure agreements.
  • A) bucolic
  • B) arcane
  • C) artless
  • D) desultory
  • E) diffident
66. Climbing the corporate ladder at a cutthroat private equity firm proved to be an ________ struggle that ruined his work-life balance.
  • A) artless
  • B) bucolic
  • C) arduous
  • D) desultory
  • E) diffident
67. The candidate’s ________ admission that he had zero administrative experience endeared him to working-class voters tired of polished lies.
  • A) arcane
  • B) artless
  • C) arduous
  • D) desultory
  • E) dilatory

  • A) copious
  • B) ascetic
  • C) cosmopolitan
  • D) cacophonous
  • E) anomalous
69. A quick public apology by the airline CEO did little to ________ the anger of stranded holiday travelers.
  • A) assuage
  • B) canonize
  • C) coalesce
  • D) contend
  • E) polarize

  • A) betrayed
  • B) coalesced
  • C) assuaged
  • D) burgeoned
  • E) canonized

  • A) cacophonous
  • B) bucolic
  • C) dilatory
  • D) desultory
  • E) diffident

  • A) abated
  • B) burgeoned
  • C) abjured
  • D) censured
  • E) equivocate

  • A) bucolic
  • B) cacophonous
  • C) cogent
  • D) copious
  • E) diffident

  • A) censure
  • B) canonize
  • C) contend
  • D) coalesce
  • E) polarize
75. The ethics committee voted overwhelmingly to ________ the senior partner for insider trading violations.
  • A) canonize
  • B) censure
  • C) coalesce
  • D) assuage
  • E) burgeon

  • A) antipathy
  • B) chicanery
  • C) deference
  • D) canonization
  • E) diffidence

  • A) abjure
  • B) polarize
  • C) coalesce
  • D) censure
  • E) abate
78. The defense attorney presented such a ________, data-driven argument that even skeptical jurors quickly voted to acquit.
  • A) desultory
  • B) cogent
  • C) dilatory
  • D) diffident
  • E) cacophonous

  • A) desultory
  • B) compelling

  • A) contend
  • B) assuage
  • C) abjure
  • D) canonize

  • A) desultory
  • B) copious
  • C) diffident
  • D) bucolic
  • E) dilatory

  • A) bucolic
  • B) cosmopolitan

  • A) cogent
  • B) desultory
  • C) copious
  • D) bucolic
  • E) compelling
85. The ________ intern hesitated to speak up during the strategy meeting, fearing her technical suggestions would be laughed at.
  • A) cosmopolitan
  • B) diffident

  1. B) abate — Decrease in intensity; subside or ebb[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span).

  5. B) arcane — Secret or understood by very few people[span_64](start_span)[span_64](end_span).
  6. C) arduous — Taxing, laborious, or requiring strenuous effort[span_65](start_span)[span_65](end_span).

  11. B) bucolic — Associated with tranquil, idyllic rural life[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span).
  12. B) burgeon — Grow or increase rapidly; mushroom[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  13. B) cacophonous — Raucous, discordant, and gratingly noisy[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  14. B) canonize — Deeply venerate or treat like a god[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span).
  15. B) censure — Strongly criticize, chastise, or condemn[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).

  17. C) coalesce — Bring elements together; unite or amalgamate[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  18. B) cogent — Persuasive, sound, and lucid[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  19. B) compelling — Fascinating, captivating, or powerfully convincing[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  20. A) contend — Assert, affirm, or maintain in an argument[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).
  21. B) copious — Existing in abundant supply; ample[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  22. B) cosmopolitan — Worldly, sophisticated, and urbane[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span).
  23. B) deference — High regard, respect, and veneration[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span).
  24. B) desultory — Unplanned, halfhearted, or lacking enthusiasm[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  25. B) diffident — Shy, bashful, or reserved[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  26. B) dilatory — Slow, unhurried, or intended to stall[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).

  14. C) invigorate — Provide energy or vitality to; revitalize[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).
  15. B) mitigate — Diminish negative feelings or lessen harm[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  16. A) obsolete — No longer useful; outmoded or antiquated[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  17. A) opaque — Hard to comprehend, unclear, or not able to be seen through[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).

Group 6 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. B) admonish — Scold gently, reprimand, or urge someone cautiously[span_65](start_span)[span_65](end_span).
  2. D) aesthetic — Pertaining to beauty, style, or artistic taste[span_66](start_span)[span_66](end_span).
  3. A) affectation — A pretentious, unnatural display meant to impress[span_67](start_span)[span_67](end_span).
  4. B) alleviate — Lessen the intensity, ease, or mitigate negative conditions[span_68](start_span)[span_68](end_span).
  5. B) analogous — Comparable, similar, or parallel in nature[span_69](start_span)[span_69](end_span).
  6. B) bolster — Support, strengthen, prop up, or reinforce[span_70](start_span)[span_70](end_span).
  7. A) chauvinistic — Excessively nationalistic, partisan, or biased[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span)[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  8. A) connoisseur — An expert judge in matters of taste and fine art[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  9. A) dissemble — Conceal, disguise, or misrepresent true motives or beliefs[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span).
  10. A) dogged — Tenacious, resolute, and stubbornly determined[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).
  11. A) dupe — Deceive, trick, swindle, or hoodwink[span_76](start_span)[span_76](end_span).
  12. A) empirical — Based on observable, experimental, and verifiable evidence[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  13. B) engender — Give rise to, produce, or stimulate[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  14. B) entitled — Assuming oneself to be deserving of special privileges[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  15. B) pertinacious — Stubbornly holding onto an opinion, persistent, or resolute[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span)[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  16. A) presumptuous — Brazen, bold, and overstepping boundaries[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span).
  17. C) probity — Complete honesty, rectitude, and integrity[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span).
  18. B) proliferate — Rapidly increase, snowball, or multiply[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  19. A) specious — Seemingly plausible but fallacious and false[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  20. A) spurious — Bogus, fake, and not genuine[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).
  21. B) subjective — Based on personal perspectives and feelings rather than facts[span_87](start_span)[span_87](end_span).
  22. A) subvert — Undermine, sabotage, or destabilize an established authority[span_88](start_span)[span_88](end_span).
  23. A) timorous — Timid, nervous, fearful, and lacking self-confidence[span_89](start_span)[span_89](end_span)[span_90](start_span)[span_90](end_span).
  24. A) tortuous — Full of twists, sinuous, or overly complex and convoluted[span_91](start_span)[span_91](end_span).
  25. A) tractable — Docile, easily managed, controlled, or solved[span_92](start_span)[span_92](end_span).
  26. A) transient — Fleeting, evanescent, and lasting only a short time[span_93](start_span)[span_93](end_span).
  27. A) ubiquitous — Present, appearing, or found everywhere simultaneously[span_94](start_span)[span_94](end_span).
  28. A) underscore — Emphasize, highlight, or draw attention to[span_95](start_span)[span_95](end_span).
  29. A) venal — Open to bribery, mercenary, or corrupt[span_96](start_span)[span_96](end_span).

Group 7 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. B) appease — Pacify, placate, or calm someone down by acceding to demands[span_0](start_span)[span_0](end_span).
  2. B) arbitrary — Determined by personal whim or random chance rather than reason[span_1](start_span)[span_1](end_span).
  3. A) archaic — Ancient, obsolete, or out of date[span_2](start_span)[span_2](end_span).
  4. A) clamorous — Making a loud, confused, and vociferous outcry[span_3](start_span)[span_3](end_span).
  5. A) dearth — A scarcity or lack of something[span_4](start_span)[span_4](end_span).
  6. A) explicable — Able to be accounted for, understood, or explained[span_5](start_span)[span_5](end_span).
  7. B) hyperbole — Exaggerated statements or claims not meant literally[span_6](start_span)[span_6](end_span).
  8. B) immutable — Unchanging over time or unable to be changed[span_7](start_span)[span_7](end_span).
  9. B) indefatigable — Persisting tirelessly without fatigue[span_8](start_span)[span_8](end_span).
  10. B) indolent — Wanting to avoid activity or exertion; habitually lazy[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  11. A) insular — Narrow-minded, provincial, or isolated in outlook[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  12. B) intransigent — Uncompromising, stubborn, and refusing to agree[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  13. B) intrepid — Fearless, dauntless, and adventurous[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  14. A) irreverent — Showing a lack of respect for things that are generally taken seriously[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  15. B) loathe — Feel intense dislike or disgust for; abhor[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  16. B) malign — Speak about someone in a spitefully critical or slanderous manner[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  17. B) malleable — Easily influenced, pliable, or impressionable[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  18. B) neophyte — A person who is new to a subject, skill, or belief; beginner[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).
  19. B) plastic — Easily molded, adaptable, or flexible[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  20. B) platitude — A trite, banal, or overused remark[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  21. B) prescient — Having or showing knowledge of events before they take place[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).
  22. A) pristine — In its original, unspoiled condition; immaculate[span_21](start_span)[span_21](end_span).
  23. B) reproach — An expression of disapproval, reprimand, or disappointment[span_22](start_span)[span_22](end_span).
  24. A) robust — Strong, healthy, sturdy, and capable of enduring conditions[span_23](start_span)[span_23](end_span).
  25. B) salubrious — Health-giving, beneficial, or wholesome[span_24](start_span)[span_24](end_span).
  26. B) sanction — Penalize or impose a punitive disciplinary measure[span_25](start_span)[span_25](end_span)[span_26](start_span)[span_26](end_span).
  27. A) sedulous — Showing dedication, diligence, and painstaking care[span_27](start_span)[span_27](end_span).
  28. B) soporific — Tending to induce drowsiness or sleep[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span).
  29. B) stern — Severe, strict, forbidding, or grim in manner[span_29](start_span)[span_29](end_span).
  30. A) tendentious — Expressing or promoting a strongly partisan or controversial point of view[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span).

  10. A) fastidious — Showing meticulous attention to detail; hard to please[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  11. A) feign — Pretend, simulate, or fabricate an appearance[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  12. A) furtive — Secretive, sly, surreptitious, or covert[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  13. A) hamper — Obstruct, hinder, or impede progress[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  14. A) indispensable — Absolutely essential and completely necessary[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  15. B) lament — Express deep regret, sorrow, or grief[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  16. A) myopic — Short-sighted, insular, or lacking strategic perspective[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  17. A) nonchalant — Casually calm, composed, or coolly indifferent[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  18. A) partial — Prejudiced, biased toward one party, or discriminatory[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).

Group 11 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. B) abhor — Detest, hate, or regard with deep loathing[span_31](start_span)[span_31](end_span).
  2. A) boisterous — Noisy, energetic, animated, and unruly[span_32](start_span)[span_32](end_span).
  3. B) chivalrous — Courteous, gallant, and honorable toward others[span_33](start_span)[span_33](end_span).
  4. B) churlish — Rude, bad-tempered, and lacking good manners[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span).
  5. B) clandestine — Kept secret or done secretively; furtive[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).
  6. B) complacent — Smug and uncritical satisfaction with oneself or achievements[span_36](start_span)[span_36](end_span).
  7. B) cumbersome — Large, heavy, slow, or complicated to handle[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  8. B) debilitating — Weakening, enfeebling, or impairing strength[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  9. A) deliberate — Engage in careful thought or formal discussion before deciding[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  10. B) droll — Curious or unusual in a way that provokes dry amusement[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span).
  11. A) eccentric — Unconventional, idiosyncratic, and slightly strange[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  12. A) fractious — Irritable, quarrelsome, and difficult to control[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  13. A) limpid — Clear, transparent, or easily understood[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span).
  14. B) mawkish — Sentimental in an exaggerated or cloying way[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  15. B) obeisance — Homage, deference, or gesture of deep respect[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  16. A) ostentatious — Pretentious or showy in an attempt to impress[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  17. B) panacea — A universal remedy or solution for all problems[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  18. A) perfunctory — Carried out with minimum effort or reflection; superficial[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  19. A) perilous — Full of danger or risk[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span).
  20. A) pervasive — Spreading widely throughout an area or group[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  21. B) preclude — Prevent from happening; make impossible[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  22. B) predilection — A preference or special liking for something; penchant[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  23. A) rapacious — Aggressively greedy or grasping[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  24. B) relish — Enjoy greatly or take intense satisfaction in[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  25. A) satirical — Sarcastic, critical, and mocking through humor or irony[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  26. B) sham — Bogus, fraudulent, or counterfeit[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span)[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span).
  27. A) skirt — Go around or evade a difficult issue or topic[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span).
  28. B) sluggish — Slow-moving, lethargic, or lacking energy[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).
  29. B) spartan — Austere, frugal, simple, and devoid of luxury[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span).
  30. A) truculent — Eager or quick to argue; belligerent and aggressive[span_61](start_span)[span_61](end_span).

  1. B) acrimonious — Bitter, caustic, and sharp in tone or language[span_62](start_span)[span_62](end_span).

  3. B) beneficent — Kind, charitable, and producing positive good[span_64](start_span)[span_64](end_span).
  4. A) canny — Shrewd, astute, and sharp in practical affairs[span_65](start_span)[span_65](end_span)[span_66](start_span)[span_66](end_span).
  5. A) cavalier — Showing a lack of proper concern; dismissive or offhand[span_67](start_span)[span_67](end_span).
  6. B) distressed — Experiencing extreme anxiety, sorrow, or pain[span_68](start_span)[span_68](end_span).
  7. A) dwindling — Gradually diminishing in size, amount, or strength[span_69](start_span)[span_69](end_span).
  8. B) eclipse — Surpass, overshadow, or obscure in importance[span_70](start_span)[span_70](end_span).
  9. A) encyclopedic — Comprehensive and wide-ranging in scope[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span).
  10. B) exacerbate — Make a problem, bad situation, or negative feeling worse[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  11. A) exasperated — Severely irritated, frustrated, or infuriated[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  12. B) fungible — Interchangeable and replaceable by another identical item[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span)[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).
  13. A) hackneyed — Lacking originality; trite, cliché, and overused[span_76](start_span)[span_76](end_span).
  14. A) incongruous — Out of place; clashing or discordant[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  15. A) interchangeable — Capable of replacing or being replaced by something else[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  16. A) laconic — Using very few words; concise and reserved[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  17. A) lucrative — Producing a great deal of profit; gainful[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).
  18. A) magisterial — Authoritative, commanding, or lordly[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  19. A) onerous — Involving an oppressive, taxing amount of effort[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span)[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span).
  20. A) opprobrium — Harsh criticism, censure, or public disgrace[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  21. A) parsimonious — Extremely frugal, stingy, or miserly[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  22. A) peripheral — Of secondary, minor, or tangential importance[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).
  23. A) provocative — Causing strong emotion, discussion, or controversy[span_87](start_span)[span_87](end_span).
  24. A) renounce — Formally declare one's abandonment of a claim, right, or interest[span_88](start_span)[span_88](end_span).

  11. B) dogmatic — Inflexible and insisting upon principles as incontrovertibly true[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span)[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).

  13. A) foolhardy — Recklessly daring or foolishly bold without sensible thought[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  14. B) hinder — Create difficulties for someone or something, resulting in delay or obstruction[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  15. A) impair — Weaken, damage, or diminish in strength, quality, or function[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  16. A) impugn — Dispute the truth, validity, or honesty of a statement or motive[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span).

Group 19 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) acolyte — A devoted follower, assistant, or attendant of an influential leader[span_0](start_span)[span_0](end_span).
  2. B) anoint — Nominate, dedicate, or appoint someone formally to a high office[span_1](start_span)[span_1](end_span).
  3. A) base — Morally low, dishonorable, or ignoble[span_2](start_span)[span_2](end_span).
  4. A) coercion — Compelling or forcing someone to act via intimidation, threats, or pressure[span_3](start_span)[span_3](end_span).
  5. A) coin — Invent, devise, or fabricate a new word, phrase, or concept[span_4](start_span)[span_4](end_span)[span_5](start_span)[span_5](end_span).
  6. A) cunning — Deceitfully skillful, clever, and astute at achieving ends[span_6](start_span)[span_6](end_span).
  7. A) discomfit — Make someone feel uneasy, perplexed, flustered, or embarrassed[span_7](start_span)[span_7](end_span).
  8. A) dissent — The expression or holding of opinions at variance with official policy[span_8](start_span)[span_8](end_span).
  9. A) distill — Extract the essential elements or meaning of something[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  10. A) dubious — Hesitating, doubtful, suspicious, or not to be relied upon[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  11. A) ebullient — Cheerful, exuberant, and overflowing with enthusiastic energy[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  12. A) facetious — Treating serious issues with deliberately inappropriate, flippant humor[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  13. A) fallible — Capable of making mistakes or being erroneous[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  14. A) florid — Excessively intricate, ornate, complicated, or elaborate[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  15. A) gawky — Socially or physically awkward, clumsy, and ungainly[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  16. A) inveigle — Persuade someone to do something through deception or flattery[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  17. A) jettison — Drop, discard, or abandon something no longer needed[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).
  18. A) mendacity — The condition of being untruthful; deceitfulness and dishonesty[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  19. A) munificent — Exceptionally generous, bountiful, and lavish in gift-giving[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  20. A) naive — Lacking worldly wisdom, experience, judgment, or sophistication[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).
  21. A) noble — Possessing high moral qualities, integrity, or superior character[span_21](start_span)[span_21](end_span).
  22. A) parochial — Limited in scope or outlook; narrow-minded and provincial[span_22](start_span)[span_22](end_span).
  23. A) pedestrian — Lacking inspiration or excitement; dull and ordinary[span_23](start_span)[span_23](end_span).
  24. A) prevaricate — Speak or act evasively to avoid telling the whole truth[span_24](start_span)[span_24](end_span).
  25. A) prime — Prepare, make ready, or equip something for action or operation[span_25](start_span)[span_25](end_span).
  26. A) radical — Affecting the fundamental nature of something; far-reaching and thorough[span_26](start_span)[span_26](end_span).
  27. A) recrudescent — Reviving or breaking out again after a period of dormancy[span_27](start_span)[span_27](end_span).
  28. A) temporal — Relating to worldly as opposed to spiritual affairs; relating to time[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span).
  29. A) transitory — Not permanent; lasting only for a short time[span_29](start_span)[span_29](end_span).
  30. A) viable — Capable of working successfully; feasible and practical[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span).

  4. A) discrepancy — A lack of compatibility or difference between two or more facts[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span).
  5. A) duplicitous — Deceitful, double-dealing, and intentionally misleading[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).

  7. A) fabricate — Invent, concoct, or forge false information with intent to deceive[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  8. A) glum — Looking or feeling dejected, sullen, morose, or gloomy[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  9. A) harbinger — A person or thing that announces or signals the approach of an event; an omen[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  10. A) intrinsic — Belonging naturally; essential, inborn, and inherent[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span).
  11. A) largesse — Generosity in bestowing money, gifts, or resources upon others[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  12. A) libertine — A person who behaves without moral principles or sexual responsibility[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  13. A) malfeasance — Wrongdoing or illegal conduct, especially by a public official or executive[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span).
  14. A) manifest — Display or show a quality or feeling by one's acts or appearance; demonstrate[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  15. A) minute — Extremely small, tiny, or minor[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  16. A) modish — Conforming to what is currently fashionable or stylish[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  17. A) nascent — Just coming into existence and beginning to display signs of future potential[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  18. A) perennial — Lasting or existing for a long or apparently infinite time; continually recurring[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  19. A) pious — Deeply religious, devout, or making a hypocritical display of virtue[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span)[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  20. A) providential — Occurring at a favorable time; lucky, opportune, or fortunate[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  21. A) prowess — Skill, expertise, or superior ability in a particular activity or field[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  22. A) schism — A split, division, or rupture between strongly opposed parties or sections[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  23. A) slander — Make false and damaging statements about someone[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  24. A) stalwart — Loyal, reliable, hardworking, and steadfast[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  25. A) supplicated — Asked, begged, or pleaded for something earnestly or humbly[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span).
  26. A) terse — Sparing in the use of words; abrupt, concise, and to the point[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span).
  27. A) tirade — A long, angry, and vehement speech of criticism or accusation[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span)[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).
  28. A) universal — Applicable to or done by all people or things in the world or a particular group[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span).

Group 21 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) abject — Extremely bad, unpleasant, degrading, or completely without pride[span_63](start_span)[span_63](end_span).
  2. A) amicable — Characterized by friendly goodwill and peaceable intentions[span_64](start_span)[span_64](end_span).
  3. A) animosity — Strong hostility, resentment, or active ill will[span_65](start_span)[span_65](end_span).
  4. A) averred — Stated or declared something with confidence and assurance[span_66](start_span)[span_66](end_span)[span_67](start_span)[span_67](end_span).
  5. A) barrage — An overwhelming, concentrated outpouring of questions, words, or criticisms[span_68](start_span)[span_68](end_span).
  6. A) cathartic — Providing psychological relief through the open release of strong emotions[span_69](start_span)[span_69](end_span).
  7. A) decipher — Convert into normal language; succeed in understanding or interpreting[span_70](start_span)[span_70](end_span).
  8. A) delusion — An idiosyncratic belief or impression that is firmly maintained despite evidence to the contrary[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span).
  9. A) dispense — Distribute or manage without; get rid of or do away with[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  10. A) eloquent — Fluent, persuasive, and beautifully articulate in speech or writing[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  11. A) enthrall — Capture the fascinated attention of; charm or captivate[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span).
  12. A) eradicate — Destroy completely; put an end to[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).
  13. A) fledgling — New, inexperienced, or emerging[span_76](start_span)[span_76](end_span).
  14. A) fortitude — Courage in facing pain, adversity, or difficulty with endurance[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  15. A) fortuitous — Happening by a lucky chance; fortunate and accidental[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  16. A) goad — Provoke or annoy someone so as to stimulate some action or reaction[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  17. A) imminent — About to happen; impending and unavoidable[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).
  18. A) incontrovertible — Not able to be denied, disputed, or questioned[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  19. A) itinerant — Traveling from place to place, especially for work[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span).
  20. A) magnanimous — Generous or forgiving, especially toward a rival or less powerful person[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span).
  21. A) meritorious — Deserving praise, reward, or esteem[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  22. A) mutiny — An open rebellion against the proper authorities, especially by soldiers or sailors[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  23. A) paradoxical — Seemingly absurd or self-contradictory, but perhaps true[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).
  24. A) perseverance — Persistence in doing something despite difficulty or delay in achieving success[span_87](start_span)[span_87](end_span).
  25. A) render — Cause to be or become; make[span_88](start_span)[span_88](end_span).
  26. A) repertoire — A stock of skills, techniques, or pieces that a person habitually uses[span_89](start_span)[span_89](end_span).
  27. A) resilient — Able to withstand or recover quickly from difficult conditions[span_90](start_span)[span_90](end_span).
  28. A) resolute — Admirably purposeful, determined, and unwavering[span_91](start_span)[span_91](end_span).

Group 24 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) assailed — Attacked violently or criticized intensely[span_67](start_span)[span_67](end_span).
  2. A) benevolent — Well-meaning, kindly, and charitable[span_68](start_span)[span_68](end_span).
  3. A) berate — Scold or criticize someone harshly and at length[span_69](start_span)[span_69](end_span).
  4. A) buoyant — Cheerful, optimistic, and resilient[span_70](start_span)[span_70](end_span).
  5. A) buttress — Reinforce, support, or strengthen a structure or argument[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span).
  6. A) condone — Accept, forgive, overlook, or allow morally questionable behavior[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  7. A) contravene — Violate, breach, or go against a law, treaty, or principle[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  8. A) denounce — Publicly declare to be wrong, corrupt, or evil[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span).
  9. A) despotic — Characteristic of an absolute, oppressive, tyrannical ruler[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).
  10. A) deviate — Depart from an established course, accepted standard, or norm[span_76](start_span)[span_76](end_span).
  11. A) disinterested — Impartial, objective, and unbiased[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  12. A) escalate — Increase rapidly in intensity, scale, or extent[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  13. A) exorcise — Drive out or rid oneself of an evil spirit, painful memory, or fear[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  14. A) finicky — Fussy, fastidious, and demanding about small details[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).
  15. A) foil — Frustrate, thwart, or prevent efforts or plans from succeeding[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  16. A) intertwined — Closely linked, twisted, or connected together[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span).
  17. A) inundate — Overwhelm, deluge, or flood with a large volume of things[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span).
  18. A) ironclad — Impossible to contradict, weaken, or change; unbreakable[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  19. A) jeopardize — Put someone or something into a situation in which there is danger of loss or harm[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  20. A) mercurial — Subject to sudden, volatile, or unpredictable changes of mood[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).
  21. A) oblivious — Not aware of or not concerned about what is happening around oneself[span_87](start_span)[span_87](end_span).
  22. A) perpetrated — Carried out or committed a bad, illegal, or harmful action[span_88](start_span)[span_88](end_span).
  23. A) plaintive — Sounding sad, mournful, and sorrowful[span_89](start_span)[span_89](end_span)[span_90](start_span)[span_90](end_span).
  24. A) poignant — Evoking a keen sense of sadness, regret, or deep emotional resonance[span_91](start_span)[span_91](end_span).
  25. A) quiescent — In a state or period of inactivity, dormancy, or rest[span_92](start_span)[span_92](end_span).
  26. A) reiterate — Say or state something again several times for emphasis[span_93](start_span)[span_93](end_span).
  27. A) subside — Become less intense, violent, or severe; settle down[span_94](start_span)[span_94](end_span).
  28. A) subsumed — Included or absorbed something under a comprehensive rule or category[span_95](start_span)[span_95](end_span).
  29. A) surmount — Overcome a difficulty, challenge, or obstacle[span_96](start_span)[span_96](end_span).

Group 25 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) adept — Highly skilled, proficient, or expert in an area[span_0](start_span)[span_0](end_span).
  2. A) adverse — Unfavorable, harmful, or contrary to one's interests[span_1](start_span)[span_1](end_span).
  3. A) appropriate — Take possession of something, often without permission[span_2](start_span)[span_2](end_span).
  4. A) archetype — A standard, quintessential example or original model[span_3](start_span)[span_3](end_span).
  5. A) articulate — Able to speak fluently, clearly, and expressively[span_4](start_span)[span_4](end_span).
  6. A) auspicious — Indicating future success, favorable, or fortunate[span_5](start_span)[span_5](end_span).
  7. A) bereft — Deprived of, lacking, or sorrowful through loss[span_6](start_span)[span_6](end_span).
  8. A) captious — Tending to find fault or raise petty objections[span_7](start_span)[span_7](end_span).
  9. A) conclusive — Decisive, putting an end to doubt or question[span_8](start_span)[span_8](end_span).
  10. A) conspire — Secretly work or plot together to commit an unlawful act[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  11. A) delineate — Describe, portray, or outline with sharp precision[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  12. A) disentangle — Free from tangle, confusion, or complication[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  13. A) exhort — Strongly urge, encourage, or caution someone[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  14. A) frailty — Weakness, delicateness, or moral imperfection[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  15. A) grievance — An official complaint or resentment over unfair treatment[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  16. A) harangue — A lengthy, loud, and aggressive tirade or lecture[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  17. A) ploy — A cunning plan or action calculated to gain an advantage[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  18. A) poise — Graceful balance, self-assurance, and composed dignity[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span)[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  19. A) pomposity — Self-important, arrogant, and pompous behavior[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  20. A) proxy — A person or instrument authorized to act on behalf of another[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).
  21. A) relent — Abandon a harsh stance or surrender to request; soften[span_21](start_span)[span_21](end_span).
  22. A) rhetoric — Language designed to persuade or impress, often lacking substance[span_22](start_span)[span_22](end_span).
  23. A) rigor — Thoroughness, strictness, or demanding harshness[span_23](start_span)[span_23](end_span).
  24. A) sparse — Thinly dispersed, scattered, or meager[span_24](start_span)[span_24](end_span).
  25. A) steadfast — Resolutely firm, loyal, and unwavering[span_25](start_span)[span_25](end_span).
  26. A) suspect — Regarded with distrust, suspicious, or doubtful[span_26](start_span)[span_26](end_span)[span_27](start_span)[span_27](end_span).
  27. A) tedious — Monotonous, tiresomely dull, and slow[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span).
  28. A) vitality — The capacity to live, develop, and project vibrant energy[span_29](start_span)[span_29](end_span).
  29. A) whimsical — Playfully quaint, fanciful, or unpredictably erratic[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span).
  30. A) yield — Surrender, give way to pressure, or produce a result[span_31](start_span)[span_31](end_span).

Group 26 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) apprehension — Anxiety, fear, or dread that something bad will happen[span_32](start_span)[span_32](end_span).
  2. A) ardent — Enthusiastic, passionate, or deeply devoted[span_33](start_span)[span_33](end_span).
  3. A) axiomatic — Self-evident, unquestionable, or taken as a given truth[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span)[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).
  4. A) cease — Stop, conclude, or come to an end[span_36](start_span)[span_36](end_span).
  5. A) conducive — Making a certain situation or outcome likely or possible[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  6. A) corporeal — Relating to a person's physical body rather than spirit[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  7. A) doctrinaire — Dogmatic, rigidly uncompromising in theoretical beliefs[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  8. A) eclectic — Deriving ideas, taste, or style from a broad, diverse range of sources[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span).
  9. A) equanimity — Mental calmness, composure, and evenness of temper[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  10. A) exorbitant — Unreasonably high, excessive, or inflated[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  11. A) fickle — Changing frequently, especially as regards loyalties or tastes[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span)[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  12. A) figurative — Departing from a literal use of words; metaphorical[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  13. A) flustered — Agitated, confused, or uncomfortably nervous[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  14. A) gullible — Easily persuaded to believe something; credulous[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  15. A) idiosyncratic — Relating to an idiosyncratic peculiarity or individual trait[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  16. A) incidental — Occurring merely as an accompaniment or secondary factor[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span).
  17. A) ingrained — Firmly fixed, deep-rooted, and difficult to change[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  18. A) insolent — Showing a rude and arrogant lack of respect[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  19. A) lampoon — Publicly criticize someone or something using ridicule or sarcasm[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  20. A) lavish — Bestow something in generous, extravagant, or excessive quantities[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  21. A) lugubrious — Looking or sounding mournful, gloomy, and dismal[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  22. A) macabre — Disturbing and horrifying because of involvement with or depiction of death[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  23. A) morose — Sullen, ill-tempered, and gloomy[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span).
  24. A) officious — Assertive of authority in an annoying, domineering, or meddlesome way[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span).
  25. A) ramification — A consequence of an action or event, especially when complex[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span).
  26. A) serene — Calm, peaceful, and untroubled[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).
  27. A) supplant — Supersede and replace through force or innovation[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span)[span_61](start_span)[span_61](end_span).
  28. A) tacit — Understood or implied without being stated aloud[span_62](start_span)[span_62](end_span).
  29. A) transcend — Be or go beyond the range or limits of[span_63](start_span)[span_63](end_span).
  30. A) treatise — A written work dealing formally and systematically with a subject[span_64](start_span)[span_64](end_span).

  10. A) excoriate — Criticize or censure someone severely and harshly[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span).

  13. A) heady — Intoxicating, exhilarating, or potent in effect[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span)[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  14. A) imperturbable — Unable to be upset or excited; calm and serene[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).

Group 29 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) abscond — Leave hurriedly and secretly, typically to avoid detection or arrest[span_31](start_span)[span_31](end_span)[span_32](start_span)[span_32](end_span).
  2. A) apogee — The highest point in the development of something; climax or culmination[span_33](start_span)[span_33](end_span).
  3. A) aspersions — Attacks on the reputation or integrity of someone or something; slander[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span).
  4. A) bawdy — Dealing with sexual matters in a comical, coarse, or risqué way[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).
  5. A) chagrin — Distress or embarrassment at having failed or been humiliated[span_36](start_span)[span_36](end_span).
  6. A) colluding — Cooperating in a secret or unlawful way in order to deceive others[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  7. A) commiserate — Express or feel sympathy and pity for someone[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  8. A) conflagration — An extensive, destructive fire that destroys a great deal of property[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  9. A) contretemps — A minor dispute or an unexpected and unfortunate occurrence[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span)[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  10. A) conviction — A firmly held belief or opinion[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  11. A) croon — Hum, sing, or speak in a soft, low, gentle voice[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span).
  12. A) depose — Remove from office suddenly and forcefully[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  13. A) detente — The easing of hostility or strained relations, especially between countries[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  14. A) dowdy — Lacking smartness or elegance; unfashionable and dull[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  15. A) echelon — A level or rank in an organization, profession, or society[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  16. A) ennui — A feeling of listlessness and dissatisfaction arising from lack of excitement[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  17. A) expatiate — Speak or write at length or in considerable detail[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span)[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  18. A) fraught — Filled with or likely to result in something undesirable; tense[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  19. A) fulcrum — The point or central principle on which something turns or depends[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  20. A) imbroglio — An extremely confused, complicated, or embarrassing situation[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  21. A) jocund — Cheerful, lighthearted, and merry[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  22. A) languish — Lose or lack vitality; grow weak or feeble; suffer neglect[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  23. A) nadir — The lowest point in the fortunes of a person or organization[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span).
  24. A) nimble — Quick and light in movement or agile in thought[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span)[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span).
  25. A) ominous — Giving the impression that something bad or unpleasant is going to happen[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).
  26. A) outlandish — Looking or sounding bizarre, strange, or unfamiliar[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span).
  27. A) propitious — Giving or indicating a good chance of success; favorable[span_61](start_span)[span_61](end_span).
  28. A) prurient — Having or encouraging an excessive interest in sexual matters[span_62](start_span)[span_62](end_span).
  29. A) sadistic — Deriving pleasure from inflicting pain, suffering, or humiliation on others[span_63](start_span)[span_63](end_span).

Group 30 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) aberrant — Departing from an accepted standard, normal type, or expected behavior[span_65](start_span)[span_65](end_span).
  2. A) abide — Accept, act in accordance with, or tolerate a rule or decision[span_66](start_span)[span_66](end_span)[span_67](start_span)[span_67](end_span).
  3. A) bravado — A bold manner or a show of boldness intended to impress or intimidate[span_68](start_span)[span_68](end_span).
  4. A) callow — Inexperienced, naive, and immature[span_69](start_span)[span_69](end_span).
  5. A) capitulate — Cease to resist an opponent or an unwelcome demand; surrender[span_70](start_span)[span_70](end_span).
  6. A) cogitate — Think deeply about something; meditate or ponder[span_71](start_span)[span_71](end_span).
  7. A) deportment — A person's behavior, manner of standing, or conduct[span_72](start_span)[span_72](end_span).
  8. A) extemporize — Compose, perform, or produce something without preparation; improvise[span_73](start_span)[span_73](end_span).
  9. A) factious — Relating to or inclined to create dissension, internal division, or discord[span_74](start_span)[span_74](end_span).
  10. A) fallow — Inactive, uncultivated, or dormant for a period of time[span_75](start_span)[span_75](end_span)[span_76](start_span)[span_76](end_span).
  11. A) feint — A deceptive or distracting movement, typically during a fight or contest[span_77](start_span)[span_77](end_span).
  12. A) flagrant — Conspicuously or glaringly offensive, scandalous, or blatant[span_78](start_span)[span_78](end_span).
  13. A) gratuitous — Uncalled for; lacking good reason; unwarranted[span_79](start_span)[span_79](end_span).
  14. A) grovel — Act in an obsequious way in order to obtain someone's forgiveness or favor[span_80](start_span)[span_80](end_span).
  15. A) indecorous — Not keeping with good taste and propriety; improper[span_81](start_span)[span_81](end_span).
  16. A) intrigue — The secret planning of something illicit or detrimental; underhanded scheming[span_82](start_span)[span_82](end_span).
  17. A) nominal — Existing in name only; or very small and far below the real value[span_83](start_span)[span_83](end_span)[span_84](start_span)[span_84](end_span).
  18. A) obdurate — Stubbornly refusing to change one's opinion or course of action[span_85](start_span)[span_85](end_span).
  19. A) obstreperous — Noisy, unruly, and difficult to control[span_86](start_span)[span_86](end_span).
  20. A) odious — Extremely unpleasant; repulsive or detestable[span_87](start_span)[span_87](end_span).
  21. A) plucky — Having or showing determined courage in the face of difficulties[span_88](start_span)[span_88](end_span).
  22. A) precocious — Having developed certain abilities or proclivities at an earlier age than usual[span_89](start_span)[span_89](end_span).
  23. A) remuneration — Money paid for work or a service; financial compensation[span_90](start_span)[span_90](end_span).
  24. A) slovenly — Messy, untidy, careless, or slipshod in execution[span_91](start_span)[span_91](end_span)[span_92](start_span)[span_92](end_span).
  25. A) soliloquy — An act of speaking one's thoughts aloud when by oneself, especially in a play[span_93](start_span)[span_93](end_span).
  26. A) spurn — Reject with disdain, contempt, or scorn[span_94](start_span)[span_94](end_span).
  27. A) stolid — Calm, dependable, and showing little emotion or animation[span_95](start_span)[span_95](end_span).
  28. A) temerity — Excessive confidence or boldness; audacity[span_96](start_span)[span_96](end_span).
  29. A) tenuous — Very weak, slight, or unsubstantial[span_97](start_span)[span_97](end_span).
  30. A) verve — Vigor, spirit, enthusiasm, and animated liveliness[span_98](start_span)[span_98](end_span).

Group 31 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) abrogate — Repeal, revoke, or formally do away with a law or agreement[span_0](start_span)[span_0](end_span).
  2. A) aghast — Struck with overwhelming shock, horror, or amazement[span_1](start_span)[span_1](end_span).
  3. A) apprise — Inform, notify, or make someone aware of something[span_2](start_span)[span_2](end_span).
  4. A) beguile — Charm, enchant, or mislead someone, often deceptively[span_3](start_span)[span_3](end_span).
  5. A) boon — A timely benefit, blessing, or helpful advantage[span_4](start_span)[span_4](end_span).
  6. A) callous — Emotionally hardened, unfeeling, or insensitive to others[span_5](start_span)[span_5](end_span).
  7. A) coddle — Treat with excessive indulgence, pampering, or overprotective care[span_6](start_span)[span_6](end_span).
  8. A) crescendo — A gradual increase in loudness or the culminating peak of intensity[span_7](start_span)[span_7](end_span).
  9. A) extenuating — Making guilt or an offense seem less serious by providing mitigating context[span_8](start_span)[span_8](end_span).
  10. A) frenetic — Fast, energetic, wild, and uncontrolled[span_9](start_span)[span_9](end_span).
  11. A) fringe — Unconventional, extreme, or existing on the outer margins of a group[span_10](start_span)[span_10](end_span).
  12. A) hapless — Unfortunate, unlucky, or cursed by ill fortune[span_11](start_span)[span_11](end_span).
  13. A) immaculate — Spotlessly clean, pure, or free from flaws or errors[span_12](start_span)[span_12](end_span).
  14. A) obfuscate — Deliberately make something unclear, obscure, or confusing[span_13](start_span)[span_13](end_span).
  15. A) ossify — Cease developing; turn into bone or become rigidly fixed and resistant to change[span_14](start_span)[span_14](end_span).
  16. A) pastiche — An artistic work that imitates or combines styles from various different sources[span_15](start_span)[span_15](end_span).
  17. A) perspicacious — Having keen mental perception, insight, and discernment[span_16](start_span)[span_16](end_span).
  18. A) ponderous — Slow, clumsy, and heavy because of great weight; painfully dull[span_17](start_span)[span_17](end_span).
  19. A) recluse — A person who lives a solitary life and avoids society[span_18](start_span)[span_18](end_span).
  20. A) retaliate — Make an attack or return an assault in kind; take revenge[span_19](start_span)[span_19](end_span).
  21. A) rhapsody — An intensely emotional, ecstatic expression of feeling or enthusiasm[span_20](start_span)[span_20](end_span).
  22. A) serendipitous — Occurring or discovered by chance in a happy, beneficial way[span_21](start_span)[span_21](end_span).
  23. A) shirk — Avoid or neglect a duty, responsibility, or obligation[span_22](start_span)[span_22](end_span).
  24. A) sinecure — A position requiring little or no work but yielding financial benefit[span_23](start_span)[span_23](end_span).
  25. A) sinuous — Having many curves, turns, or bends; winding[span_24](start_span)[span_24](end_span).
  26. A) sordid — Involving ignominious actions and motives; dirty or squalid[span_25](start_span)[span_25](end_span).
  27. A) stanch — Stop or restrict the flow of something, such as liquid or loss of funds[span_26](start_span)[span_26](end_span).
  28. A) surfeit — An excessive amount of something; surplus or overabundance[span_27](start_span)[span_27](end_span).
  29. A) ulterior — Existing beyond what is obvious or admitted; intentionally concealed[span_28](start_span)[span_28](end_span).
  30. A) voluble — Speaking fluently, incessantly, and at great length[span_29](start_span)[span_29](end_span).

Group 32 Answer Key & Explanations

  1. A) abstruse — Difficult to understand; obscure, esoteric, or arcane[span_30](start_span)[span_30](end_span).
  2. A) auxiliary — Providing supplementary or additional help and support[span_31](start_span)[span_31](end_span).
  3. A) caricature — Exaggerate features or qualities for comedic, grotesque, or satirical effect[span_32](start_span)[span_32](end_span).
  4. A) depravity — Moral corruption, wicked perversion, or extreme wickedness[span_33](start_span)[span_33](end_span).
  5. A) dilettante — A person who cultivates an area of interest without real commitment or deep knowledge[span_34](start_span)[span_34](end_span).
  6. A) effrontery — Insolent, brazen, or audacious behavior[span_35](start_span)[span_35](end_span).
  7. A) encroach — Intrude gradually on a person's territory, rights, or time[span_36](start_span)[span_36](end_span).
  8. A) endow — Provide with a quality, ability, or permanent fund or income[span_37](start_span)[span_37](end_span).
  9. A) entreat — Ask someone earnestly or anxiously to do something; plead or beseech[span_38](start_span)[span_38](end_span).
  10. A) gregarious — Fond of company; sociable, friendly, and outgoing[span_39](start_span)[span_39](end_span).
  11. A) indictment — A formal charge or serious accusation of a serious crime[span_40](start_span)[span_40](end_span).
  12. A) indignant — Feeling or showing anger or annoyance at what is perceived as unfair treatment[span_41](start_span)[span_41](end_span).
  13. A) ineluctable — Unable to be resisted, avoided, or escaped; inevitable[span_42](start_span)[span_42](end_span).
  14. A) inquisitive — Eager for knowledge, curious, or inquiring[span_43](start_span)[span_43](end_span).
  15. A) latitude — Scope for freedom of action or thought[span_44](start_span)[span_44](end_span).
  16. A) levity — Treatment of a serious matter with humor or in a manner lacking due respect[span_45](start_span)[span_45](end_span).
  17. A) malevolent — Having or showing a wish to do evil to others[span_46](start_span)[span_46](end_span).
  18. A) mediate — Intervene between people in a dispute in order to bring about an agreement[span_47](start_span)[span_47](end_span).
  19. A) occlude — Stop, close up, or obstruct an opening, orifice, or passage[span_48](start_span)[span_48](end_span).
  20. A) pacify — Quell the anger, agitation, or excitement of; bring peace to[span_49](start_span)[span_49](end_span).
  21. A) paragon — A person or thing regarded as a perfect model of excellence[span_50](start_span)[span_50](end_span).
  22. A) patronize — Treat with an apparent kindness that betrays a feeling of superiority[span_51](start_span)[span_51](end_span).
  23. A) penurious — Extremely poor or poverty-stricken; miserly[span_52](start_span)[span_52](end_span).
  24. A) piquant — Stimulating, pleasantly sharp, engaging, or exciting to the mind[span_53](start_span)[span_53](end_span).
  25. A) rampant — Flourishing or spreading unchecked, especially of something unwelcome[span_54](start_span)[span_54](end_span).
  26. A) remote — Having very little connection with or very unlikely to happen[span_55](start_span)[span_55](end_span).
  27. A) reprobate — An unprincipled or wicked person; morally degenerate[span_56](start_span)[span_56](end_span).
  28. A) turbid — Cloudy, opaque, or thick with suspended matter; muddled[span_57](start_span)[span_57](end_span)[span_58](start_span)[span_58](end_span).
  29. A) turgid — Swollen, congested, or tediously pompous and bombastic in style[span_59](start_span)[span_59](end_span).
  30. A) vacuous — Having or showing a lack of thought or intelligence; mindless[span_60](start_span)[span_60](end_span).
